    “Oh, baby,” Agatha says, voice mocking. It’s like putting on a sweater that fits just right, an old comfort to go back to talking like this, to lock away all those pesky vulnerabilities. “I know you care.”

    When they go to tribal that night, Agatha keeps her focus tight. She watches Todd, watches Wanda, watches the way his confidence never wavers until Jeff declares his idol is fake.

    It feels nice to pull off a blindside after all this. The simplicity of it. What Agatha doesn't do is look at the jury. She can’t look at Nicky out there, on the wrong side of tribal. She can’t bear to see him look at her how he always does, soft and kind.

    He probably wouldn’t look at her that way if he knew the things she said to Rio. He probably wouldn’t look at her that way if he knew how easy it was for her to cast Rio aside. He probably wouldn't look at her that way if he knew who she really was, not the woman he made up in his brain. So it’s best if Agatha doesn't look at him. According to the game, he’s dead and gone. She can face him after she wins.

    Day 33 Working with Wanda is easy enough. She’s smart enough to not be constantly annoying, and dumb enough to do what Agatha tells her to. It’s clear that Wanda has switched over to working with Agatha just to take her to the end, so Agatha will be jury poison and Wanda can win the money easy. But the problem with Wanda’s little plan is that Wanda is actually strategizing. And is a woman. Two things that, in Agatha’s experience, make a jury hate you.

    “Did you see their faces last night? When Todd pulled out that thing?” Wanda is asking now, as they walk to the reward challenge. “I get why you do it, this is fun.”

    If Wanda keeps this up, she just might be even easier than Agatha to beat at final tribal.

    “It is, isn’t it?” Agatha says. She’s not looking at Wanda when she says it, but glancing behind her at Rio, who is trudging through the sand with a glare and a frown. “Just a blast.”

    Day 34 “Agatha, please, can we talk?”

    Agatha is getting tired of this. Tragically, Wanda is off making nice with John and Abilash, which leaves Agatha alone at camp with Rio. “What is there to talk about?” Agatha asks innocently.

    Rio’s jaw clenches.

    “Are you kidding me?” she says. “It was one vote, and you completely shut me out.”

    “Oh I haven’t shut you out, darling. I’m talking to you right now. How was your day?”

    “Be serious,” Rio says.

    “No,” Agatha says with a grin.

    “You can’t just do this,” Rio lets out, with a sort of growl that Agatha would normally find hot, but now… she still finds hot, even when she’s barely able to look Rio in the eye. “You have to actually talk to me. Even when it’s hard.”

    “I don’t have to do anything,” Agatha says breezily. “Free will and all that.” “Fine,” Rio says, “you don’t have to. But, Agatha, please. It’s you and me. I’m—listen, according to the great state of Vermont you and I are… we’re connected, okay? We’re a unit. We—”

    “Are we?” Agatha snaps. “If we were a unit, it sure would have been shitty to vote out someone I cared about like that, would it have been?” Rio’s eyes harden.

    “Don’t,” she says, voice cracking a little. “I cared about him too, you know.”

    “Not like I did,” Agatha snaps. “You don’t fucking get it, Rio. You haven’t been let down since you were a little kid. You haven’t had someone who is supposed to love you most in the world just… not. But he did. And this was just one more time he was let down, disappointed by the people who were supposed to protect him.”

    “I…” Rio trails off, swallows. “I know that I’ll never get it. Not like you do. But, you can’t—voting someone out on Survivor isn’t the same thing. It sucks, but it’s not real. It’s a game.”

    Agatha shakes her head.

    “Not with him,” she says, hating the way her voice almost breaks with it. “It was different with him.”

    Day 35 “Can I ask you a question?” Wanda asks on the way to treemail. “I’d rather you didn’t,” Agatha says frankly.

    “Well, I mentioned my son to you before, and I was just—” “Gay son or straight son?”

    Wanda sighs. “Billy. His name is Billy. My other son is named Tommy.” “Wow, such interesting and unique names,” Agatha says.

    Wanda chuckles a little. She glances around. “Don’t spread it around, I want to protect their privacy, but I would rather you call him Billy than, ‘the gay one.’”

    “Sure,” Agatha says. “So what did little gay Billy say about me?” “Sorry if this is out of left field,” Wanda says, “but he would never forgive me if I didn’t ask… what exactly is the nature of your and Rio’s relationship?”

    “Oh, Wanda, we’re just very close friends.”

    Day 36 “You’re unbelievable,” Rio yells, running a hand over her face in frustration. “You can’t just whisper the vote in my ear right before tribal. You have to talk to me!”

    “Why not?” Agatha asks innocently. “It sure got Abilash out of our hair.” “Because we are supposed to be in an alliance,” Rio says. She takes the machete and whacks off some branches of a nearby tree for emphasis. So dramatic.

    “Oh, are we?” Agatha asks. “Because I think alliances don’t vote out other members of their alliance without telling.”

    Rio hits the knife into the tree trunk. Hard.

    “Are you really going to keep harping on this?” She asks. “After everything we’ve been through? One vote?”

    Something pesky catches in Agatha’s chest.

    “One vote? Is that all he was to you?”

    “You know that’s not what I meant,” Rio snaps, voice rough. “The only reason I voted for him was because I knew his fate was sealed. It was awful writing his name down, and believe me, I really fucking wish I hadn’t. I would change it in a second if I could.”

    There’s something in her voice, a desperation, a rawness, that makes Agatha look up at her, right here in the very jungle where they’ve spent so many nights.

    “Really?” She says, her own voice just as raw.

    “Of course,” Rio says gently. “Even if it tanked our game, I wouldn’t have voted for him.” She steps closer, close enough that she can brush some hair behind Agatha’s ear. Agatha leans into her hand. “But Agatha… he still would have gone home.”

    Agatha pulls back from Rio’s touch. She was close. Too close. “Well, I guess we’ll never know now,” Agatha says, clipped. “I mean, it’s just math,” Rio says, a little bit smug. “I don’t know how many more times I have to tell you that I wasn’t the decider.” “You know what? You don’t have to tell me anymore,” Agatha says. She suddenly can’t stand to look at her, all high and mighty and logical, looking at Agatha like she’s the crazy one. “We don’t have to keep this whole act up.”

    “What whole act? Pretending we are fighting?”

    Agatha laughs. “Who’s pretending, baby? I meant this. You and me. Our ‘alliance.’ It’s top four now. Every man for himself.”

    “Are you kidding?” Rio groans. “After everything, you’re just calling it quits like that?”

    Agatha shrugs, like it’s nothing. “Maybe I am.”

    Rio’s jaw sets. Her hand clutches the knife. “You know,” she says, slow and purposeful and dangerous, “maybe it’s a good thing Nick is gone. Because now he won’t have to see you like this.”

    “Keep his name out of your fucking mouth,” Agatha spits. Rio always was so good at starting fires and she’s started one deep in Agatha’s chest, flames of pure hot rage. “I will never be in a fucking alliance with you again, Rio Vidal.”

    “I wouldn't even if you asked,” Rio spits back.

    “So we’re settled then,” Agatha says, almost calm. It’s easier now. No complications. “You and me, we are on our own until the end.” “Perfect,” Rio says. There are tears in her eyes.

    Agatha looks away.

    Day 37 Rio still votes with them, even after everything. Even after sitting at tribal with a scowl on her face and answering Jeff in one word answers, Rio writes down John’s name alongside Wanda and Agatha, cementing their top three.

    Agatha smiles when the votes are read. When it’s down to just the three of them. She’s one step closer to the end.

    But she still can’t look at the jury.

    Day 38 “Are you ready,” Jeff bellows, like he’s a fucking WWE announcer and not a man under six feet tall who takes a sick pleasure in watching people starve, “for your final immunity challenge of the season?” Agatha has never been more ready in her life. She needs this season to be over. She needs to leave this fucking island. She needs to leave this island a million dollars richer.

    Here’s how Agatha sees it. If Wanda wins immunity, she’ll clearly take Agatha to the end. That’s why she’s been playing nice, any idiot could see. If Rio wins immunity, which is tragically likely, odds are pretty good that she will also take Agatha. All Agatha needs to do is just grovel for approximately two minutes, and maybe take off her shirt, and she’ll have Rio eating out of the palm of her hand.

    Agatha can pretend to forget about the pit of betrayal in her stomach that rises like acid whenever she looks at Rio, if it means making it to the end. Because she will be making it to the end, god damn it.

    “This will be a battle for your lives,” Jeff is saying. “First, you need to navigate a labyrinth, stopping at four different stations for puzzle pieces—” Agatha rolls her eyes. Final immunity used to have some fucking class. Some simplicity. See how long you can touch a pole. How long you can remain above water. How long you can balance. But now it’s a dumb maze and puzzle, like any other challenge.

    Agatha, however, is very good at puzzles.

    Sure enough, after the three of them get themselves sweaty and dusty and panting from the maze, Agatha stands between Wanda and Rio, desperately trying to make pieces connect to build a three dimensional flame. “We all know fire represents life out here,” Jeff commentates in the background. “And never more so than this, with whoever completed their fire puzzle first, getting a seat at final tribal. Wanda is making good progress, having come from behind in this challenge, but Agatha still has a strong lead. Rio dominated in the first half of this challenge but seems too distracted by what Agatha is doing to work on her own puzzle.” Agatha laughs to herself. Then she tunes Jeff out, tunes Rio out, tunes Wanda out. She looks at the pieces by color. Sees which ones have an oblong shape, which ones are more square. She thinks of Nicky’s swift hands, the way he would fill in the gaps she left open in each puzzle they worked on together.

    But now he’s not here to help her. Now she has to do it herself, lining up the pieces and connecting them, with only her own two hands.

    Agatha has always been very good at relying on only herself. And this challenge is no exception, as her fingers slide each piece into place until there’s only three more, then two, then one, then—

    “Agatha wins immunity!” Jeff declares, and there it is, the necklace around her neck again, the final piece of security. Agatha doesn’t need either of these people to bring her to the end. She’s bringing her god damn self to the end.

    And one other person. That she gets to choose.

    Instead of following the Survivor tradition of holding court with the two losers who don’t have immunity, Agatha goes for a swim when she gets back to camp. She jumps off the rocks at Nicky’s cove and forces herself deep under the water, deep enough to forget this whole game, to forget the choice she has to make tonight, to forget that in two days she is going to leave Survivor beach and not know what home to go to.

    Agatha swims until the sun is almost down, until production shows up at the cove to remind her that tribal is imminent. Like she didn’t already know. Halfway back to camp, she’s accosted by the far too energetic form of Wanda, clear panic on her face. She’s panicked for good reason. Agatha would like nothing more than to send Rio packing tonight, but there’s no denying that Rio is far easier to beat than Wanda.

    “Tell gay Billy it’s nothing personal,” Agatha says with a slightly too aggressive pat to Wanda’s shoulder.

    Wanda, surprisingly, just laughs.

    “If I lose to anyone,” she says, “he’ll be happy it’s to you. Listen, I know you’re going to vote me out. I know you and Rio are… close.” Agatha rolls her eyes. This type of conversation is exactly what she wanted to avoid “But,” Wanda continues. She sniffs a little. Oh, this bitch is good. “If this past year has taught me anything, it’s that nothing is more important than family.” Agatha wonders if she can successfully run and jump back into the ocean instead of hearing about the dead husband again. “I would do anything for my boys, and if you were in my position I know you would too. If I had—if I knew what Nick meant to you, I wouldn't have voted him out. Gameplay be damned.”

    At this, Wanda grabs Agatha’s arm and forces her to look in her eyes. “But I guess…” Wanda’s voice hitches, then becomes stronger. “I guess Rio didn’t feel the same.”

    Her green eyes fill with with tears. If Agatha’s guts weren’t folding in on themselves at this bitch knowing Agatha like this, at the truth in her words, Agatha would be impressed.

    “So you’re not even going to grovel?” Agatha asks Rio, as they pick up their bags to go to tribal.

    Rio turns to Agatha, like she’s shocked Agatha is speaking to her. Her jaw is tight.

    “No,” she says plainly. “You’re smart, Agatha. You know that you have a better chance of beating me than her.”

    It’s what Agatha had thought herself, but still, she wants to fight Rio on this, fight Rio’s smugness that she thinks she’s the patron saint of objectivity in a game where it’s impossible to be objective. “Oh, I don’t know about that,” Agatha says, “Wanda might be just as much of a manipulative bitch as I am.”

    “Oh Agatha,” Rio says, grinning, “ no one could be as much of a manipulative bitch as you are.”

    Despite everything, affection seeps through Rio’s words, like she just can’t help herself.

    Agatha, unable to help herself either, laughs.

    Rio grins at her, wide and gap toothed and oh, Agatha missed that smile. “You know,” Rio says slowly, like she’s approaching a stray cat, “there’s always the plan. It’s not too late.”

    Oh, but it’s far too late. And Rio should know that.

    “Agatha,” Jeff announces once they sit down at tribal, “how does it feel to know that you will be guaranteed a spot at final tribal tomorrow night?” “I feel on top of the world, Jeffrey,” Agatha lies through her teeth. She does not look at the jury.

    “And have you already decided who you are taking to the end?” “A lady never tells,” Agatha says with a grin.

    “Wanda, Rio,” Jeff tees up, “any final case you want to make?” “It’s funny,” Wanda says, “people build up Agatha as such a villain, but for the back half of this game, she’s been more loyal than I have. If she wants to sit next to someone she can beat, well, I voted off almost every single one of the people sitting on the jury.”

    “So did I,” Rio snaps. “And I did it with Agatha.”

    “I don’t think you were with Agatha on every vote,” Wanda says casually. “If my math is correct.”

    Rio growls a little. God, if Agatha does vote off Wanda tonight, she might not be able to keep her hands to herself back at camp with this angry and feral version of Rio.

    “Don’t listen to her,” Rio says, low and hard to Agatha. “She’s just pitting us against each other.”

    “I actually don’t think Wanda has anything to do with that,” Agatha snaps at her. She’s tired Rio’s know-it-all attitude. She’s tired of the whiplash of wanting Rio out of her sight and wanting Rio. She’s tired of Wanda’s meddling, of having to decipher what’s real and what’s not real. She’s tired of this game.

    “With that,” Jeff says, “it is time to vote. Wanda, Rio, you would only be able to vote for each other so your votes will cancel each other out. Agatha, it’s all you. You’re up.”

    “Music to my ears,” Agatha says.

    She’s so fucking tired. So tired that she slips. So tired that when she stands up to vote, her eyes slide over to the jury.

    And there he is. He’s a little more put together, hair combed back and pulled into a ponytail, wearing the teenage boy combination of a button down shirt with board shorts and flip-flops. And he’s smiling. Nick Scratch is smiling at her, big and real and full of an affection that Agatha has been aching for over the last week.

    Rio’s words from the other day ring in her ears. Maybe it’s a good thing Nick is gone. Because now he won’t have to see you like this. Something that feels half like tears and half like vomit rises in Agatha’s throat as she approaches the voting station, Rio’s words in the back of her mind, Nicky’s smile in the front of it. She pictures tomorrow, Nicky on the jury, Rio beside her, Nicky looking directly at another couple that fractured because of him.

    He doesn’t deserve that. He doesn't deserve any of this. He deserves to be up here more than she does, more than Rio does, more than Wanda does. Did Rio know this would happen? When she cast her vote for Nicky? Did she know that Agatha would be up here, pen in hand, paralyzed at the idea of this boy looking at her with anything but love in his eyes. Like the waves crashing up on the rocks out here, Agatha’s rage at Rio suddenly spikes and sprays up. It’s all Rio’s fucking fault. They could be in this together, the two of them, united at the end like they had always planned, if it wasn’t for her stray fucking vote. Agatha wouldn’t have to be agonizing over this; over which choice will hurt Nicky less. Well, Agatha is fucking done with it. She’s done with all of it. She thinks her hand might shake, that she might be unsteady with it, but it’s surprisingly easy.

    “Are you sure?” Rio asked all those months ago, in a Vermont courthouse, eyes looking up at Agatha with blind adoration.

    “Of course I’m sure,” Agatha said, reaching out with one hand to hold onto Rio, while the other hand picked up a pen and signed her name on a marriage license, sure and confident.

    And now, with that same surety and confidence, she picks up a pen and votes Rio out of this game.

    Day 39 Agatha has always found it funny how Jeff and runs the finale like it's a mock trial. Opening statements, then witness interrogation, then closing statements and a jury vote.

    It’s all very silly. Except, of course, the million dollars awaits the winner of this fake court.

    “Look,” Agatha says in her opening statement. It’s directed toward the Todds and the Johns and the Kates of the jury. It is not directed toward the far side, where Nick and Rio sit. Agatha is playing her role to a T today, and both of them have the unique power to break her wide open, to cause everything to spill from her, a machete to a coconut.

    “I’m not going to mince words,” she says to the other six of them. “I’m not the nicest, I’m not the most honest, I am what many people on this very game have labeled a villain. I’ve had a target on my back since Day One. And yet I’m here. I’ve outwitted, outplayed, and outlasted to be sitting in front of you. And in my humble opinion, that is far more impressive than someone who came in here with no reputation, no baggage. I understand if you might not like me. You might even like Wanda more than me. There’s no accounting for taste. But I played this game better than anyone else here, and I deserve the win. Easy as that.”

    She gets some nods, some smiles. Kate whispers something to Abilash and he grins. Maybe this will go over better than last time.

    But then it’s Wanda’s turn.

    “I live with a 13-year-old Survivor expert,” Wanda says, with an easy grin, “so I know all the statistics. Did you know that a fan has never won any of the fan vs. favorites seasons so far? Because the returning players come with relationships already built in, with their tribemates and the people who become the jury. Coming in as a fan is a disadvantage, no matter what my opponent says. But I’ve managed to make it here. Coming off the hardest year of my life, I made it to final tribal of Survivor. Yes, in the process I’ve lied to some of you that I’ve come to care about, but I’ve always been clear about one thing: my family is the most important thing to me. And I will do whatever it takes to give them something good. Of course we need the money, of course it could change everything, but even more than that, I want my boys to know that their mother can do anything, that I will scale walls and dive into the ocean and eat bugs for them. That I will win Survivor for them.”

    What a loser. Sarah and John are both crying a little. Jesus fucking Christ. It goes that way for most of tribal, as each jury member comes up to ask a question and Wanda answers all of them with her grieving mother shtick and Agatha answers them honestly.

    (“What would you do with the money?” Kate asks. Which is rich coming from her, Agatha can smell when someone comes from money. “Pay for my kid’s college,” Wanda says sweetly, “maybe redo the bathroom we’ve been meaning to for years.”

    “Buy a boat,” Agatha says.)

    But then it shifts. Then Jeff says, “alright, Nick, you’re up.” And Nicky stands up, walks over to them, board shorts and flip flops and grin and all. He looks a little nervous to ask his question, eyes flicking back to the jury.

    “Hi!” Nick says cheerily. “Just want to say, you both really deserve top two, and I feel lucky to have played this game with you.” He says the last part looking directly into Agatha’s eyes. She feels her throat close up. “My question is a little basic, but I just wondered if you could share with me any regrets you’ve had about how you played the game.” “Hey, Nick,” Wanda says kindly, before Agatha even has a chance to jump in. “I don’t know if this is the answer you want to hear, but honestly I can’t say I regret anything about how I played. Sure, maybe we didn’t need to use the fake idol on Todd (sorry Todd!), but overall, every choice that I made led me here. Sitting at final tribal. So I can’t say I regret any of them.” “Cool,” Nick says, with a grin, “good answer.”

    Agatha wonders what it must be like, to be born with such kindness. Then Nick turns to her.

    “How about you Agatha? Any regrets?”

    She had planned to go a similar route as Wanda, some bullshit answer about no regrets, but seeing him right now, so sweet and innocent, makes her go a more truthful route.

    “I regret that I couldn’t save you,” Agatha says, voice scratchy, “that I couldn’t take you to the end.”

    Nick sighs, his smile growing softer. “Agatha,” he says. “You couldn’t… you didn't need to…” Then he swallows, looks at the jury again, before turning back to Agatha. “You’re sure that’s it? Nothing else?” Agatha tilts her head, like he’s speaking another language. What else could there be?

    “Nick,” Wanda says, “you’re sweet. But I don’t think she’s going to say what you want her to say.”

    “What is that supposed to mean?” Agatha says, turning to Wanda. Wanda lifts up her hands like Agatha is somehow being aggressive, like Agatha isn’t holding back from telling Wanda to stay the fuck out of her business.

    “Well, I don’t want to assume,” Wanda says, clearly enjoying assuming, “but I think Nick wanted you to express some regret about what you did to Rio.”

    “Don’t speak for him,” Agatha snaps, because it’s easier than processing Wanda’s words. Easier than processing the truth of them.

    “I mean, she’s not entirely wrong,” Nicky says sheepishly, “but I don’t want to put words in your mouth. I just wanted to see if there—if there was anything you wanted to say about last night… you know what, it doesn’t matter. That’s my question, I’m gonna sit back down.”

    Agatha wants to go up, wants to go after him, wants to tell him that it’s not his fault, none of it is, that some people just don’t have as much room in their hearts as he does. But she doesn’t, staying rooted to the stupid fake tree trunk she’s sitting on.

    “Look,” Wanda says, facing the jury, still fucking talking for some reason. “Nick, I think you’re an incredible kid, I genuinely hope my boys turn out like you when they’re older. I think you see the best in everyone, but you can’t make someone into something they’re not. We’ve been dancing around it all night, but Agatha Harkness has the reputation she has for a reason. She will stop at nothing to win. Including voting out not only her Day One, but the woman she was allied with in a former season. You can’t force her to regret that.”

    “And what the fuck do you know about it?” Agatha says, turning to Wanda, color rising in her face. Like she knows anything about her and Rio. “I know more than you think,” Wanda says smugly. “I spent most of the game on a tribe with you and Rio, and I watched every episode of Solomon Islands. I watched how you cast everyone out except Rio. I really thought you had loyalty to one person. But you don’t, do you? When you voted Rio out last night, it showed that you really are the villain they say you are.” Agatha hardens. It’s easier than breaking.

    “Agatha and I actually had a falling out back at the merge,” Rio chimes in from the jury, bringing up their stupid defunct plan, somehow still defending Agatha.

    “Oh please,” Wanda says, “we all know that was staged.”

    “You don’t know the first thing about me,” Agatha says, “or how I play this game.”

    Wanda laughs, easy and cold at the same time. “Beg to differ,” she says, “you have played this game with all strategy and no loyalty.” Agatha bares her teeth. “Oh, look at you all high and mighty. Like you didn’t vote out your own alliance.”

    “I didn’t vote out my Day One,” Wanda says coolly. She looks at the jury, at where Nick and Rio sit together, then turns back to Agatha, eyes steely. “And I didn’t vote out my Survivor son.”

    Agatha feels ice harden in her veins. “Neither did I!” She practically screams, all strategy abandoned, a crack of emotion in her voice. Nicky can’t think she voted for him. He can’t.

    “She didn’t!” Rio says again from the jury.

    Rio needs to shut the fuck up. She needs to stop defending Agatha, stop reminding Agatha just how much she cares.

    “I don’t need your help,” Agatha hisses at Rio. Rio flinches back a little. “And I don’t need your vote.” Rio flinches back even more. Good. “Oh, I think you do,” Wanda says, smiling like a snake about to pounce on its prey. “I think your little girlfriend is the only jury vote you’re going to get. Not even sweet Nicky.”

    Before she knows it, Agatha is up on her feet.

    “Don’t you fucking dare talk about him like that,” Agatha snarls. Her hands unbidden, go for Wanda’s throat.

    Wanda dodges, still grinning.

    “It’s funny, you know,” she says, “Nick wanted you to regret that Rio vote so badly. And I think you will. But I think you’ll regret it because Rio didn’t have the balls to show the jury who you really are.” “Like you’re any better,” Agatha growls. Her hands go for Wanda’s shoulders now, shoving her so she falls off the stump.

    “Whoa!” Jeff says, leaping to his feet, “let's all simmer down.” “Yeah, Agatha,” Wanda says from the ground, still smiling. “Maybe you should simmer down.”

    Then she grabs Agatha’s leg and drags her down with her.

    “Come on guys, chill!” Jeff is yelling, as he steps in between them, separating them. “We don’t do this on Survivor. We use our words.” “Pussy,” Agatha mutters.

    “Not that word!” Jeff says.

    Agatha growls at him as she gets up to her feet, brushes the dirt off of her, shoots a glare at Wanda. Wanda glares back. But there’s else something hidden in there, that smile of victory. That she may have hit the ground first, but she knows she won. Agatha wants to slit her throat.

    “Okay, now ladies,” Jeff says, patronizing as always. Agatha wants to slit his throat too, for good measure. “Can we finish this tribal without being at each other's throats?”

    “No guarantees,” Agatha says, at the same Wanda says, “sorry, Jeff.” Fake bitch.

    “Good,” Jeff says, ignoring Agatha. He takes in a deep breath. “Okay, Rio you’re up.”

    How perfect.

    When Rio stands before Agatha and Wanda, her face is set. After spending every single day with Rio for a full calendar year, Agatha has gotten damn good at reading her. Agatha can tell by the set of Rio’s mouth when she’s hungry, when she’s angry, when she’s horny, when she’s all three. But now, there’s nothing. Rio’s face is a blank canvas as she looks back and forth between Agatha and Wanda, until her eyes settle on Agatha. “All of that,” she says flatly. “These last five minutes… this whole embarrassment of a final tribal and you still don’t regret voting me out, do you?”

    “Oh my god,” Agatha says, throwing up her hands. “It was one fucking vote.”

    ”Are you kidding me?” Rio explodes. Gone is her stony expression; her teeth are bared, her eyes are wild, her face is flushed. She’s beautiful when she’s angry. A goddess who is about to smite Agatha where she sits. “You sending me home was ‘one fucking vote,’ but a vote I cast that wasn’t even the deciding factor is enough for you throw everything we had away? Jesus Christ, Agatha, you make it so hard to be on your side.”

    “Tough shit,” Agatha hears herself growl. “You should have known what you signed up for.”

    “You’re right,” Rio snarls. “I should have known better.” Then she turns and walks away. Agatha wants to run after her, wants to punch her in her beautiful face, then drop to her knees and beg for her to take Agatha back.

    But Agatha stays put. She can’t let herself break. Not when the whole world is watching.

    “Well, okay,” Jeff says, chuckling nervously. “It is time to vote.” So they vote. The eight people who hold Agatha’s fate in their hands vote, and Agatha can feel the win slipping away with every one of them. She wants to throw up, wants to run away, wants to dive into the ocean and never come up. All of this to lose? All of this to be humiliated on nation TV again?

    Last time, the same resentment and embarrassment stirred in her gut, but every time she started to feel the hot shame of coming this far only to lose, Rio was there, with a joke or a touch or a smile, a reminder that Rio didn’t care if she won her lost, Rio still wanted her.

    She’s not so lucky this time.

    Agatha watches, numb, as Jeff teases the results and doesn’t give them, running off to some helicopter while the contestants are left to their own devices. Last time, the minute the cameras stopped rolling, Rio was by her side. This time, she doesn't even look at Agatha.

    Agatha watches, as if from a distance, as the camera crew puts their equipment away, as the contestants talk and laugh with each other, like this whole night didn’t happen.

    “Hey.”

    Agatha has to hold back a childish sob. There, standing behind in front of her stump, is Nicky, still looking at her with kind eyes, somehow. “Hey,” Agatha says back, voice rough with it.

    “I don’t—” Nicky stops, takes a deep breath in. “Just tell me it’s not because of me, that you guys are fighting. I don’t want—it can’t be.” Agatha breathes out, slow and shaky, Another nightmare come to life. “Nicky,” she says softly. “Some things just aren’t meant to last.” Then, for the first time since she’s known him, Nicky’s eyes grow harder. “I don’t think that’s true. Not if you try. Not if you really love each other.” “Nicky—”

    Nicky just sighs. “I’m tired, Agatha. I want—I’m just gonna go to sleep, eat something. You should too. It’s been a long night.”

    And then he leaves her, walking toward the rest of the contestants. Toward Rio. Leaving Agatha utterly and completely alone.

    Reunion Five.

    Five separate times.

    That’s how many times in the past six months Agatha has gotten into the clunker of a car she sometimes steals from her neighbor and driven the four hours up from Boston to Vermont, down a familiar winding road to a familiar modest home on a familiar sprawling farm, only to turn around and drive back, unable to face what waits for her inside.

    Agatha hates Boston in the fall. The college students back in the city just to be loud and unaware of how to share the sidewalks, tourists stopping to take pictures of leaves, the chill in the air that signifies a miserable winter ahead. The fact that Agatha is back here, when last fall was curled up in a warm bed with Rio, tuning the rest of the world out.

    Well, the rest of the world sure as hell exists in this miserable city. And Agatha fucking hates it.

    But not as much as she hates the thought of seeing Rio again. The only reason Agatha hauls her ass to South Station to get on a train to New York to film the goddamn Survivor reunion is because it’s in her contract. The things one does for a dollar.

    Agatha shows up to the CBS studios early. She’s familiar enough with the hallowed halls of a failing broadcast television network, that it’s easy for her to find her way around, to dodge the PA who is supposed to be escorting her. She watches the crew rush and fumble, tripping over wires and struggling to corale the audience. It’s peaceful somehow, to watch these people panic over their unimportant jobs.

    After the reunion, Agatha doesn't drive up to Vermont anymore. It’s childish to think that anything would come of it.

    Day One —Rio’s on the beach.

    Chapter 16: Jeff Probst and the Terrible,

    Day 39 This is absolutely not Jeff Probst’s first rodeo. Jeff has done this. Jeff has done this almost 50 times. He has seen people weep, seen people fist fight, seen people declare war and confess their love and propose marriage. All at final tribal council.

    That’s what, in his not-so-humble opinion, makes Survivor the best damn show on television. No one just goes quietly into that dark night. They come back, and make people face the consequences. And Jeff gets to watch it all. And 99% of the time, he fucking loves it. She has a thick Russian accent, which is oddly soothing to someone raised on Rocky and Bullwinkle cartoons. “This Lilia you keep talking about?” “No!” Jeff snaps, feeling his ears go warm. Our editors are already cutting together a fantastic narrative, a love story between a beloved villain and the woman they call Death? It’s pretty perfect.”

    “Check on you,” Charlie corrects. “But yeah, we also just want to run the jury order by you.”

    He hands Jeff a piece of paper. Jeff snatches it from Charlie and skims, even though he already knows what’s going to be on it.

    “Really,” he sighs, “we’re ending with Agatha? Isn’t that a little predictable? Why don’t we just do her first, get it out of the way? Maybe end with someone like… I don’t know, Lilia, who I’m sure will end tribal on an interesting and insightful note.”

    Charlie just gapes at Jeff. “Interesting and insightful? Jeff, this is reality TV.”

    “My point stands.”

    Charlie sighs, stands. “This is the lineup for tonight, okay? This is the lineup and then you’re off the hook for months. Maybe take a vacation, relax, unplug.”

    “To where?” Jeff sneers. “A tropical fucking island?”

    “Fine,”Jeff snaps. “Now get the hell out of my trailer.”

    “Billy, Jen, Rio—”

    Night 39 “Welcome to final tribal council!”

    Jeff seems a little tense.

    Rio can’t blame him. It’s been a long season. But she is enjoying how the vein in his neck pulses, the way his eyes flash but his forehead doesn't budge.

    If Rio’s being honest with herself, it’s easier to focus on which parts of Jeff Probst’s face have movement in them then what is going to happen in about five minutes.

    “In just a few minutes,” Jeff says. “I’ll bring in the jury, eight people who you had a hand in voting out.”

    It’s different from this side. Being at final tribal with dirt still under her fingernails and the same shirt she’s been wearing for a month. Different to be the person vying for the vote instead of the one casting it. Rio has to sit up here and make a case to win this whole stupid thing, to tell these eight people why they should give her a million dollars and not her competitors. To tell them that she played the game of Survivor better than anyone else out here.

    There are two problems with this. One, Rio has barely been playing the game of Survivor this time around. She’s lucky her competitors are mostly idiots, because maybe ten percent of her brain has been devoted to the game.

    The other 90%—well, that leads Rio to the second problem with final tribal. How the ever loving fuck is she supposed to focus on this stupid game when she is about to see Agatha again? When Agatha is going to walk onto this set looking like the beautiful and devious woman that Rio fell in love with seven years ago and never fell out of love with. Looking like the woman who took Rio’s face in her hands and kissed her so hard last night that Rio forgot years of resentment and bitterness and loneliness. Well, no, it’s not that she forgot, it’s that she remembered. Remembered that the reason this woman caused Rio so many years of misery is because the moments where they were together felt bigger than anything in the world. That the spark that exists when Agatha Harkness wants her burns brighter than any heartbreak.

    Rio would love to simply have one conversation with Agatha, to be as drunk and innocent as she was back on that Applebees boat all those years ago and ask, “what are we?”

    But Rio is going to have to talk about Survivor. Who the fuck cares about Survivor when Agatha Harkness is here in the flesh?

    “And let’s welcome in our jury,” Jeff is saying distantly. “Sharon, Shaun, Monica, Jimmy, Darcy, Alice, Lilia, and Agatha, voted out at last night’s tribal council.”

    Rio’s eyes track every jury member as they come in, eyes glossing over each forgettable face until they land on the most memorable face Rio has ever seen.

    Agatha walks into final tribal like the heroine of an Austen novel walks into a ball. Every head turns. Every conversation ceases. Because what would there be to talk about or to look at that is not this sight. Agatha always commands attention, but tonight it’s even more so. Rio feels her spine straighten, her eyes widen, her lips forming an involuntary smile when she sees her; Rio is the bumbling gentleman brought to silence by the most beautiful woman at the ball.

    Agatha is clean. Agatha is clean and her shoulders are exposed and her hair is somehow still messy and she’s smiling and she’s clearly not wearing a bra and all of those individual things hit Rio like a freight train. But nothing quite so much as what hits her when Agatha looks at her. Agatha looks at her with those same sharp, bright blue eyes that first saw Rio on Solomon Islands. And then, so quick that the cameras and other contestants don’t catch it, she winks at Rio.

    And Rio, if she was in one of those old novels, would simply swoon. But she is not in a romance novel from the 19th century. She is on the long running reality television series Survivor.

    Jeff is droning on about something in the background, Rio isn’t paying attention. She’s watching Agatha, and Agatha is watching her right back. Maybe this will be it. Maybe she will get to get to watch Agatha all night, the color in her cheeks, the shadow of the dip in her collarbone, the way her shirt falls over her chest, the way—

    “Rio!” It’s Jeff.

    “Huh?” Rio says eloquently.

    “Jesus Christ,” Jeff mutters, “alright, we are going to do this again. And you three are going to pay attention.”

    “I’ve been paying attention, Jeff,” Billy says. Suck up.

    The title of sole survivor and the million dollar check that goes along with it is absolutely not what Rio wants to win tonight. Her eyes shift to Agatha again. Agatha is watching her.

    Please, Rio wants to say, please let it all be real this time. Let it last beyond this game. Please, please, please.

    “Alight,” Jeff says, “let’s get started.”

    Night 39 Jeff is as tightly wound as Jen has ever seen him. It’s quite frankly, hilarious.

    But Jen needs to focus. She has a game to win.

I think it’s the most interesting and fascinating game in the world. It exposes truths about humanity that nothing else does. So, of course, playing this game has taught me so much about myself. I came into this game defining myself by the players I love, including of course, my mother. Including my favorite player of all time, who I can’t believe is sitting on the jury right now.” Suck up. “But I’d like to think, at this point, I am a player in my own right. I’ve made big moves, I’ve made tough choices, I’ve made allies and enemies, and I am genuinely so excited to break it down for you all. And, oh my god, to be sitting here next to these two incredible women—it’s an honor of a lifetime.”

Who played Survivor best. And no offense to the two people sitting next to me, but the choice is pretty obvious.”

I could last 39 more days out here.” Rio’s eyes again shift over to Agatha, who is watching Rio like she is a fresh Dominos pizza after a month of starvation. “But I don’t want to,” Rio says, more to Agatha than anyone else. “I want to go home.     In Which Billy Breaks His NDA Billy comes home skinny.

    Skinnier than usual, that is. Billy has always been smaller than average, since he came out of Wanda essentially half the body weight of his brother. Since then, he’s grown from a concerningly tiny baby to a gangly boy to— in his words—a “skinny legend.”

    But it’s different when Billy comes home from his stint on Survivor. When Billy comes home from Survivor, he looks like someone who has been starving on a desert island. Which, of course, he has.

    Wanda crushes him in a hug the second he comes through the door, and it almost hurts how easily she can lift him up, how small his arms are around her, like he is nine instead of 19.

    Billy is grinning at her, his big boyish smile, and suddenly Wanda knows. With that big grin and his starving body—she knows without a doubt that her son made it far. That he made it to the end.

    Nick almost jumps out of his seat when actual customers come in, two guys probably a few years younger than him, brothers, by the look of it, the same flop of curls on their heads, even though one is ripped as hell and the other one looks like he could barely lift one of the surfboards they sell here. “Let me know if you need help finding anything,” Nick calls from the counter.

    Nick prides himself on finding the silver linings in most things. For example, the good thing about being on Survivor is that the first few years Nick had this job, people signed up for his surf lessons immediately, making a connection in their minds that because he was on a TV show set on an island, he could do anything in the water. He made an insane amount of tips and cemented himself as someone who knew what the hell he was doing.

    The bad thing about being on Survivor is everything else. It took getting slightly famous for about three months for Nick to realize that he does not take well to fame. He doesn’t like that people can say hateful things on the internet about him, and even worse about people he competed with. He doesn't like that when he’s meeting someone new, all they want to do is tell him how he could have won a game show he absolutely could not have won.

He gets that the internet exists, he gets that anonymity is impossible when he went on national TV at 19 and now has his face plastered on the shop’s social media. But still, Nick would sometimes prefer to just live in a cabin in the woods and not talk to anyone, like certain other people who competed on the same TV show.

There’s the occasional queer or trans kid who softly confesses that seeing Nick on TV was so special, and Nick can’t help but melt a little, can’t help but feel the burn of tears in the back of his throat that that blur of a confusing time actually meant so much to these strangers. It’s a stark reminder of how much it meant to him at the time.     “Hmm?” Nick says. He clears his throat.

    “This season of Survivor,” Billy says. “Not because I’m on it. But because she is.”

    “Oh, I don’t know,” Nick says, trying to strengthen his voice.     Billy grins. “I’m my mother’s son.”

Kind of annoying, but in a charming way. Not worthy the energy of envy.

    “You are indeed,” Nick says, and it comes out too softly. “Look,” Billy says, “I promise I’ll let you get back to work after this, but just… just watch it. From one Agatha Harkness fan to another… you’re gonna love it, Nick.”

    “I’ll think about it,” Nick says with a soft smile. It’s the best he can do. “Okay,” Billy says, “I’m going to stop being annoying now.” “Not possible!” His brother calls.

    Billy rolls his eyes. “Also I’m going to buy my brother a surf lesson.” “It’s the least you can do!”

    Nick laughs. He spends the afternoon teaching straight son—Tommy, he finds out eventually—how to surf, and gets the joy of the salt air in his lungs, the joy of sharing this thing he loves so much with someone else. When he gets back to dry land, back home, he pulls up his phone. After Survivor, they were given everyone’s contact info. He saved Agatha’s number as a hope more than anything else. He thinks about texting her, about saying you’ll never guess who I saw today. Then, he sighs and puts his phone down. Six years is too long for just a text. And Nick can’t bear to think about the possibility of her not responding.

    He puts it out of his mind. He puts it out of his mind for months and months until one day it’s suddenly, Wednesday, February 26th, at 7:55pm. Then, in a moment of bravery, Nick decides to do what a wise woman told him to do many years ago, and follow his curiosity.

    He turns on Survivor.

    In Which Rio and Agatha Both Break Their NDAs “Honey, you gotta come see this!”

    Rio ignores the shouting from the living room and looks at the same piece of paper that has been driving her into an early grave for the last hour. “I’m a little busy,” she calls back.
