    The Night Before the Reunion “How much longer?” Jen asks.

    Her calves are fucking killing her and she’s out of breath in a way that would be embarrassing if she was breathing actual air and not the smog that makes up Los Angeles’ atmosphere.

    “You sound like a child,” Lilia says from just ahead.

    Jen rolls her eyes.

    “I saw that,” Lilia says.

    From Jen’s right, Alice laughs into the warm evening air. “You two are ganging up on me,” Jen grumbles.

    “Well, only one of us is going to be a million dollars richer tomorrow,” Alice says with a grin. “So suck it up.”

    Jen doesn't acknowledge Alice’s comment, just like she has ignored the dozens of similar comments since last fall.

    She can’t count on it. She can’t walk around like she’s won a million dollars, because there is a very good chance she hasn’t. This is fucking Survivor they are talking about. How many times in the history of the game has the clear favored winner been overtaken by someone else, someone more male or more white or more straight? (Okay, that last one isn’t really an option with this top three, but still.)

    Neither of Jen’s hiking companions seem to realize this though, acting like Jen has already won, just 24 hours before she finally knows. Just 24 hours before the maybe that has been overtaking Jen for nearly nine months now will finally be resolved.

    Jen was planning on spending the night before the reunion holed up in her hotel room, charging minibar purchases to CBS, but Lilia insisted they go up Westridge trail to see the sunset while they were in LA. “You know, you won’t combust if you don't see the sun set over the Pacific,” Jen kindly ribbed her at the bottom of the hill, but she was grateful for something to do.

    Sure, she may be actively sweating like a pig, but it’s still nice to be up here. It’s nice that Lilia wanted to go on this hike with just Jen and Alice. It’s nice that the bonds made on a set in Fiji can actually transfer to real life. That didn’t happen the first time Jen played Survivor. Even the idea of seeing the faces of her fellow competitors after Cayman Islands felt like a cruel reminder of those who made it to the endgame without her. But this time feels different. Maybe it’s because Jen did make it to the final three with all her limbs intact. Or maybe it’s because she played the game with these specific people. Maybe it’s because every week without fail, Lilia clips Jen’s horoscope from the newspaper and sends it to her in the mail, so that Jen has the unique experience of opening a letter to find out that romance was in the air for Taurus two weeks ago. Maybe it’s because, once the show started airing, Billy made a group text to send the best reactions to their season, with mostly him and Darcy sending what deranged people on the internet were saying about them. And every week, without fail, Rio tries to leave the group chat and is immediately added back in.

    Or maybe it’s because of what has been unfolding between her and Alice over the last several months. Starting that first night after final tribal, when Jen and Alice were up so late talking that they were the only ones still awake when production dragged Agatha and Rio back into Poderosa, with harsh words that clearly had no effect.

    “What do you think they were doing?” Alice asked in a low voice. Jen turned to her to give a withering are you fucking stupid glare, only to see Alice grinning at her. And then Jen was the one who felt fucking stupid in the face of that knowing smile, like Alice knew more than just what Agatha and Rio were up to, like Alice’s smile contained a wealth of knowledge that Jen desperately wanted to dive into.

    So Jen did. Jen found that after the second round of Survivor, she was compelled to dive right into whatever she wanted, without fear of falling and twisting anything. She went out to dinner more. She went on spontaneous day trips just for the thrill of being somewhere new. She went dancing. She went on bad dates. She went on good dates. And one of those good dates happened to be with Alice. And another one. And another one. And then there were no more bad dates, but she was still trying going out to dinner and going on day trips and going dancing, only now she was doing those things with Alice. So much so that Jen almost forgot they were on Survivor together, they had done so much as a pair that the island seemed less and less significant with every passing day.

    It turned out that Lilia was annoyingly right—romance was in the air for Taurus.

    “So how compatible are Taurus and Sagittarius?” Jen asked Lilia over the phone, a few months into whatever was happening between her and Alice. Not that she believed in astrology, but Lilia’s belated horoscopes were annoyingly spot on.

    “It depends,” Lilia said, a knowing smile in her voice. “Are you asking me about the compatibility of Taurus and Sagittarius or are you asking me about the compatibility of you and Alice?”

    Fucking Lilia.

    “Here we are,” Lilia says now, as they reach the crest of the hill. “Oh damn,” Jen says, before she can help it. Lilia, once again, is right. The sun setting over the Pacific is a sight to behold, whether they are in Fiji or the Palisades.

    The sky streaks down pink and orange on the water, and some of it finds its way to the faces of three women on a mountain.

    “Shit,” Alice says, leaning on the fence to look as closely as she can, “that’s what I call a million dollar view.”

    “Don’t,” Jen warns.

    “Come on,” Alice says, grinning that knowing grin, even brighter by the orange of the setting sun. “We all know you have it. You have two votes right here.”

    “We don’t know anything,” Jen insists. “And you two need to stop jinxing it.”

    “I have said nothing,” Lilia says peacefully. “I simply want to watch the sun set one more time, before my dear friend comes into significant wealth.” “Fuck off,” Jen says. “And speaking of significant wealth, are you giving us the details of the rich guy who wants to take you to Europe?” It’s Lilia’s turn to look away, back to the sunset.

    “It’s really lovely out here, you know,” she says.

    “Lilia,” Alice says, “just give us a name.”

    “It’s an old fling,” Lilia says, waving a hand. “One that I’m afraid meant far more to him than it did to me.”

    “Oh, so what,” Jen says. “If he’s bankrolling a ski trip or whatever, just do it.”

    “I am not the kind of woman who skis,” Lilia says with disdain. “Yeah but you would definitely have something to say about how the sunlight hits the snow or whatever,” Alice says.

    “I think you like having this guy into you,” Jen says, thrilled the attention is off her.

    “Please,” Lilia says, but she doesn't deny it.

    “I get it,” Alice says kindly. Then she shoots Jen a look that is anything but kind, a sort of quiet mischief in her eyes that makes Jen’s stomach pleasantly fold in on itself. “I mean Jeff really was a hottie back in the day.” “Age doesn’t make one less attractive,” Lilia says, seamlessly taking the bait. “Just because he got some work done, it doesn’t mean that… oh, fuck both of you.”

    Jen laughs, unable to help herself. “Just like how Jeff Probst is going to fuck you in that ski resort in Sweden,” she asks innocently. “Jennifer,” Lilia scolds.

    “Am I wrong?” Jen presses, grinning.

    The pink of the sunset highlights the pink in Lilia’s cheeks. It would be sweet if the situation wasn’t so insane.

    “It’s nothing,” Lilia says, waving a hand, “he’s just an old flame.” “Just an old flame,” Jen repeats, astonished. “Jeff fucking Probst?” “I can’t believe we are going to see him tomorrow,” Alice says, “I can’t believe that Jeff is going to be giving you fuck-me eyes while handing Jen a million dollars.”

    “Alice, please,” Lilia says.

    “Alice, please,” Jen echoes.

    Alice just laughs, the victory of having pissed both of them off in one go. Jen watches her, watches the easy teasing between these two women that Jen can’t believe she didn’t know a year ago. If she was more sentimental, less cynical, Jen would say something about how this moment—the air cooling around her, the sun sinking down over the ocean, these two people surrounding her—that alone is worth a million dollars.

    But Jen isn’t a fucking idiot. She wants the damn money.

    The Morning of the Reunion “I can’t believe they call this a farmers market,” Rio says for maybe the eighth time in a five minute period.

    She is wearing sunglasses and a tank top while scowling at the dozen or so vendors at Los Angeles’ incorrectly named Original Farmers Market. The sun is bright on her bare arms and her hand is firm in Agatha’s and the combination of her grip and her complaining is the only thing that is making Agatha feel at all tethered to the ground in this moment. “I actually think,” Rio is still ranting, “that if you bulldoze an actual farm to make an outdoor mall, and then put your trendy little restaurants on the other half of the land, you don’t deserve to call it a farmers market.” One of the vendors looks up at Rio, clearly overhearing her. A brief look of hurt crosses his face. Agatha breathes out a laugh. It’s just the pick me up she needs.

    “What’s so funny?” Rio asks.

    “Not anything you’re saying,” Agatha assures her, patting Rio’s bicep right on the skull. “I think these charlatans would faint if they ever saw a real farm.”

    “Thank you!” Rio says.

    “We can take a different route back to the hotel after breakfast,” Agatha assures her, “wouldn’t want you to have a full breakdown about semantics.” “You’re making fun of me,” Rio says.

    “Always,” Agatha says, giving Rio’s bicep skull a quick kiss. Predictably, Rio forgets what she was about to say. God, Agatha doesn’t remember how she lived six years without this woman eating out of the palm of her hand.

    “Oh my god,” a loud voice sounds from across the market. “Are you two…?”

    Agatha turns to see a young woman gawking at them. She looks to be in her early 20s, but somehow learned to dress only yesterday. Her phone is out, held in her hand, which is sporting a variety of rings.

    “Aw,” Agatha says, “a fan.”

    ”Absolutely not,” Rio says, with a fire in her voice that makes her complaints about the farmers market seem friendly.

    ”You don’t want to say hi?” Agatha asks innocently.

    “Come on,” Rio says, not bothering to answer. She tugs on Agatha’s hand, leading them away from the fashion-averse little lesbian, and through the throngs of vendors.

    “You know,” Agatha says, “jealousy isn’t a good look on you.” It’s a blatant lie and they both know it. Jealousy is an amazing look on Rio. “It’s not jealousy,” Rio snaps, “we just can’t be seen as a couple yet.” “Sure,” Agatha says, “like one dyke with an iPhone is going to make CBS sue us.”

    “You never know.”

    Agatha laughs. “Oh, honey, I know. Look at the press requests alone. Look at the ratings. We are CBS’ bread and fucking butter this season and you know it. Speaking of press requests—”

    “I am not doing Watch What Happens Live,” Rio says, still tugging Agatha along, though the fan is far behind them.

    “Oh come on, baby,” Agatha says, lowering her voice a little, “we both know I can convince you to do anything. So why pussyfoot around it?” Rio finally stops walking and turns back to Agatha. She lowers her sunglasses and grins.

    “We also both know the convincing is the fun part, don’t we?” Agatha, against her will, grins back. She’s tempted to drag Rio back through what is or is not a farmers market to their hotel and start the convincing early. But she knows she can’t do that, she knows— “Anyway, this is the place,” Rio says. She shifts a little from one foot to the other.

    Agatha looks up at the facade of a diner, squatly situated between the street and the market, just a quick walk from their hotel, just down the block from CBS Studios.

    It felt like a good place to meet when they were cementing plans a few weeks ago. Casual, public, convenient. But now, the restaurant looms over Agatha like a tidal wave.

    Rio’s hand is still in hers, firm and steady.

    “Do you want me to come in with you?” she asks. “Or give you some time to…”

    Agatha shrugs. She wants Rio to put her sunglasses back up, to hide the care in her deep brown eyes.

    “Whatever,” Agatha says, shrugging a shoulder. “It’s just a breakfast.” Rio raises her eyebrows.

    “Sure, Agatha,” she says flatly, but still gentle. “Just a breakfast.” Agatha resists the urge to look away.

    Come in with me, she wants to say, I know I should face him alone but I just don’t want to. Come be my bodyguard against any old wounds, kissing them shut if they should open.

    But Agatha doesn’t say any of that. Instead, she pushes Rio’s sunglasses back up against her face and says, “I’m sure there is a real farmer somewhere in this dump. I saw a guy selling oranges on the corner.” “Oh yeah?” Rio says, far too kindly.

    “Yeah, I bet he also has a lot of gripes with this place. You two should link up for... maybe 15 minutes.”

    “Fifteen minutes,” Rio repeats, voice too soft. “I’ll be back in 15 minutes.” She leans forward and kisses Agatha on the cheek. Agatha is probably the strongest person in the world for not pulling Rio back toward her. Instead, stomach turning in on itself, she watches Rio walk back toward the “Farmers Market” (hate to see her leave, love to watch her walk away) until she is so small that Agatha has nothing left to do but to turn around and walk into the diner.

    It’s annoyingly classic, cheesy wallpaper and red vinyl booths, existing to evoke the very nostalgia that rises in Agatha’s throat when she sees a young man sitting at one of the booths, nursing a coffee.

    Nicky looks older, a man more than a boy, shoulders broader, arms filled out, an actual beard on his face instead of the measly scraps of hair on his chin during Survivor.

    Agatha almost turns around and leaves the diner. She should not have this feeling in her chest, breath catching and releasing all at once just from the sight of this boy (man) she knew for a smattering of weeks so long ago that he has the ability to grow facial hair and probably has amassed scores of family of friends who know him and love him far far more than— “Agatha!”

    Well, there goes that escape plan.

    But when Nicky looks up at her, brown eyes just as warm as they were seven years ago, the urge to run disappears like the steam rising from his coffee mug.

    Nicky is immediately up on his feet, half jogging toward her, and there is that excitable boy that Agatha met on an island all those years ago, still with the gap in his teeth when he smiles at her.

    It’s an out of body experience to be embraced by someone Agatha thought never wanted to see her again. All Agatha has time to register is that he is stronger than he used to be and that he smells like boy and coffee and ocean, before she is released once again and faced with that wide imperfect grin.

    “I got us a table,” Nick says eagerly, like they are any other people meeting for breakfast. “Is Rio coming?”

    “She had to see a man about some oranges,” Agatha says. “She’ll be back in 13 minutes.”

    “Specific,” Nicky says, leading her to his booth. He slides in one side while she takes the other. “I’m actually glad it’s just you here.” He plays with his napkin a little. “I wanted, um, to kind of clear the air between us.” ”Jesus,” Agatha lets out, “before I’ve even had my coffee?” She realizes a second too late that she sounds like a decorative mug owned by a lonely women in the suburbs.

    Nicky must realize it too, because a childish burst of laughter escapes him. “Don’t,” Agatha warns.

    He puts his hands up.

    “I wouldn’t dare.” Then he grins. “I would never talk to you before you had your coffee.”

    ”Ha ha,” Agatha deadpans, but she can feel the smile on her own face. ”I know you’re technically a white woman in your 50s,” Nicky says, “but I didn’t take you for the Hobby Lobby type.”

    Agatha glares at him, her chest glowing warm.

    Just then, a waitress appears, holding a coffee pot aloft. “Coffee?” She asks, monotone, and Nicky immediately falls into a fit of giggles.

    “Fill ‘er up,” Agatha says, putting on an exaggerated Midwest accent. “This stuff is fuel for me, let me tell ya.”

    It gets the desired result, Nick laughing even more. The waitress gives them a look and then moves on.

    ”God,” Nicky says wiping his eyes, “you’re so funny.”

    Agatha has to stop herself from preening a little. She is so funny. “And now that you’ve had your coffee,” he says slyly, “what I was going to say…”

    ”I feel like we’re moving out of the complimenting me phase far too quickly.”

    “…is that I’m sorry.”

    Agatha stops up short.

    “Why the hell are you sorry?” she asks bluntly.

    Nicky shrugs. He looks down at his hands.

    “I never called. I never texted. I let a stupid game in the way of—” “Shut up,” Agatha says. He blinks up at her. “Are you fucking kidding me? You let a stupid game get in the way? Nicky, I didn’t talk to my legal wife for six years. I stewed and festered and made it everyone else’s fault. I’m the fucking grown up in this situation.”

    “I’m 26–”

    “Don’t interrupt your elders.”

    He laughs a little.

    “What I’m saying,” Agatha says, and this is the hard part. This is seven years of wanting and waiting and raging all coming up at a cheesy fucking diner on the edge of a farm they turned into a mall. “What I’m saying is that I’m sorry. I said I would be there for you and I wasn’t.” “I wasn’t either!” Nicky argues, “I’m just as—”

    “No you’re not,” Agatha says. It comes out soft. Her hand reaches for his of its own accord. “Trust me, kid, you really—”

    “But I don’t—”

    “What did I say about interrupting?” Agatha snaps, half-joking. Nicky, half-joking himself, rolls his eyes and says, “okay, mom.” Suddenly the air in the diner is heavy, Agatha’s palm on Nicky’s knuckles weighted. She tries to swallow away the growing ache in her throat. It’s a joke, a joke, a joke. She knew this boy for three weeks.

    Nicky clears his throat. Agatha does the same.

    “So you’re sorry, I’m sorry, we get it,” Agatha says dismissively. She pats his hand and then quickly cracks open her menu. “You a pancakes person?” Nicky just smiles at her. Ugh.

    “Yeah, Agatha, I like pancakes. Wanna split some?”

    “Oh, so just because we made nice, now you think I don’t want my own pancakes?”

    Nicky laughs, puts his hands up. “God forbid.”

    Agatha shakes her head. “First, you talk to me before my coffee and now this. Shame on you, Nicholas.”

    He laughs again. It’s a good sound, an easy sound, the only thing light enough to clear the air.

    “So how are you feeling about tonight?” Nicky asks. “Big finale?” Agatha shrugs. She smiles. In the window behind him, she sees Rio approaching the diner.

    “Oh, tonight?” Agatha says airily. “I couldn’t care less about what happens tonight.”

    Nicky laughs again, but she’s barely joking. She knows—as Rio comes inside, stopping to give Nicky a tight hug, then sliding into the booth next to Agatha, taking her hand under the table—that the last 15 minutes matter far more than a stupid fucking game show.

    The Afternoon Before the Reunion “One hour, Mr. Probst,” the nervous PA calls, sticking his head into Jeff’s dressing room. “They’re expecting you in hair and makeup soon.” “Hey, thanks, James,” Jeff says with a smile.

    That’s right. He may be the host and EP of the most popular reality show on broadcast television, but Jeffrey Lee Probst still remembers the PAs names. He’s considerate that way.

    After James leaves, Jeff jumps up off his chair, cracks open and chugs a full Red Bull, does a quick line of coke, and then leaves the comfort of his dressing room for the bustling halls of CBS Studios.

    God, Jeff loves his job. Specifically, Jeff loves a live reunion finale. There’s nothing like it; the energy in the building, the tension coming to a breaking point, that look of joy on the winner’s face after they’ve been waiting months to find out if they won.

    There were a few years where production decided to forgo the live reunion, and Jeff would read the votes right there on the island, before giving the jury pizza and champagne and having a faux-reunion right there. It was one of Charlie’s worst ideas.

    When they finally brought back live reunions, Jeff could have flown with joy. This is what Survivor is all about.

    Everyone he passes in the hallway gets a wave. New contestants, old contestants, the light guys, the music guys.

    “Peter!” Jeff greets. “Sharon, you’re looking fabulous. Sam, long time no see, brother!”

    Oh, Jeff is on top of the world. He has just had a relaxing hiatus, in two weeks, he’s going to fly to Fiji to do it all again, and hopefully, in the interim—

    “Lilia!” Jeff can’t help but smiling wide when he sees her, walking down the halls of the studio like it’s the aisle of some health food store, unbothered and curious and gorgeous.

    For most people, Jeff just says hi without stopping—there is only one hour until showtime, and he’s a very busy man—but, for Lilia, he slows down. “Jeff,” she says, voice smooth and low. He bets she’d be a great singer. “Lilia,” he says, trying to match her tone. He’s on cloud nine right now, he’s feeling good, so he trusts his live TV instincts to think of a killer opening line. “You didn’t respond to my emails.”

    Not his best. But also, not his worst.

    Lilia chuckles a little. She watches the people go by.

    “Is this the right place to have this conversation?” she asks. Jeff shrugs. “Hey, we got about 55 minutes. And the ticket I sent you is for tomorrow.”

    A smile tugs at her mouth.

    “Come on,” he says, voice low, “no expectations. Just a free vacation with an old friend.”

    “An old friend,” Lilia repeats.

    Jeff nods. “And hey, if a repeat of Bolivia happens…”

    Lilia lightly slaps him on the arm. Which is how the whole thing in Bolivia started, if he recalls.

    “Jeffrey,” Lilia scolds. Which was the second step of the Bolivia incident. Jeff grins. Everything’s coming up Probst.

    “See you at LAX bright and early,” he says with a wink, and just has time to hear the melodic sound of her laugh before he’s back down the hallway. “What’s up, Maximoffs,” he says, an extra spring in his step. “Wanda, you know we’ve been trying to get you back. Billy, top three, my man! Billy’s brother, looking sharp!”

    Jeff gives the boys a high five and then is off toward hair and makeup. He rounds a corner to see Agatha Harkness, hand in hand with the other one. Agatha grins at him, puts her other hand up in a wave. Jeff does an about face and goes back the way he came. Nothing is killing his vibe right now. He will have to see them in 50 minutes and not a moment sooner. Turning around runs him straight into Charlie, who looks as stressed as he always does.

    “Charlie!” Jeff calls, clapping him on the back. “You ready to put on one hell of a live show, brother?”

    Charlie cracks a weak smile. “Sure am.”

    “Come on, I know you’re more excited than that! You see the ratings we’ve been getting? The people love Survivor.”

    “Yes, they do,” Charlie says. “Hey, after the reunion, let’s chat about that. About where to go from here.”

    Something in Charlie’s tone breaks through the combination of attention and uppers that are racing through Jeff’s bloodstream.

    “Why not chat now?” he asks.

    “Oh, well, you’re about to be on TV,” Charlie says, bumbling a little. “I got 50 minutes.” Jeff stands his ground, crosses his arms. “If you don’t tell me now, I’ll be stewing on it all finale. Won’t make for great TV.” Charlie sighs. “Alright, fine. Short version. The season’s doing great, the people love it, we’ve definitely got some stars.”

    “Well that’s great news,” Jeff says. “Why do you still look like you’re telling me my dog died?”

    Charlie swallows. He’s sweating.

    “Well CBS and, if I’m being honest, myself, have already thought of a great concept season for next year, where we can bring back certain players.” Jeff doesn't get the issue. This is what they do.

    “Okay,” Jeff says. “Which players?” Then, all at once, Charlie’s words break through the coke fog. “Charlie. Which fucking players?” Then, like the cold hand of imminent death, Jeff feels a grip on his shoulder. “Jeffrey,” Agatha Harkness’ voice sounds, as he tries to free himself from her wife’s clutches. “Miss me?”

    The Reunion This is the coolest day of Billy’s life. He’s staring into stage lights, his face is beat to hell from CBS’ makeup team, he has a cute little outfit on, and oh, he is sitting as one of the top three players on motherfucking Survivor. An audience is cheering at an unreasonable volume, and even through the deafening wall of sound, he can still hear his mom and his brother yelling his name.

    It’s been a whirlwind of a day. Hell, it’s been a whirlwind of a year, but today sums it up nicely. He spent most of the morning on the phone with a local gay bar, his afternoon in glam with people making each curl of his hair perfect, and the last two hours rewatching the finale with the rest of the cast, waiting for this very moment.

    “I’ll read the votes,” Jeff says.

    Billy, along with a live studio audience, holds his breath. Billy’s not stupid. He knows he didn’t win. He’s mostly made his peace with it, but there are nights when he’s stayed up late, thinking of the moves he made on the island, thinking of what he should have said at final tribal, if there was any answer that would have given him the win over what he actually said. He’s spent hours scrolling comment sections filled with, anyone but Billy, please not Billy, Billy is just a whiny nepo baby who doesn't even deserve to be on Survivor, and one particularly awful one that said I’ll think I’ll actually vomit if it’s Jen or Billy. That was the only one he reported. And if his math is correct, that person better get a bucket ready tonight.

    Because Billy might not be winning, but one of the women up here with him sure is. And he’s pretty sure it’s not Rio.

    Jeff pulls out the first vote.

    “Jen,” he reads. There are cheers.

    Billy, unconsciously, reaches for Jen’s hand. She takes it. “Rio,” Jeff reads.

    Rio, on Jen’s other side, takes her other hand.

    “Billy.”

    Billy lets out what can only be described as a gay gasp. His spare hand reaches across Jen for Rio’s. He knows it’s a gamble, Rio barely tolerates him, but in that moment, Rio takes his hand and squeezes it, no questions asked.

    If Billy’s brain was at full function, if he wasn’t caught up in adrenaline and the lights and the crowd and the energy, he would be doing calculations, who voted who, which jury member wrote down his name, what the odds are for each of them.

    But Billy, hands sweaty and being held by two women he admires more than anything in the world, can only hold his breath and wait. “Jen,” Jeff reads.

    This is it. If Billy knows anything, he knows how a vote reading goes. If Jeff reads Billy’s name or Rio’s name, then either of them have a shot. But if it’s Jen’s name, that means she’s done it.

    And sure enough—

    “Jen.”

    Jen’s eyes are shut, like she can’t bear to see this audience that is about to scream for her, like if she opens her eyes, it will have all been a dream. Billy squeezes her hand tight, as Jeff picks up the next vote and says those life changing words: “The winner of Survivor: Road to Redemption and one million dollars is… Jen!”

    Jen’s eyes spring open. There are tears in them. Thousands of people are screaming for her.

    Billy finds tears in his eyes as well. He pulls Jen into a hug, and Rio is there too, and they all hold each other for a minute before they pull back and Jen starts to smile so big it fills the whole studio.

    “Holy shit,” she breathes, and then it’s chaos. Then she’s swarmed by the jury coming in and congratulating her and hugging her, Alice lingering longer than the rest, Billy notices. Then the pre-jury players come in, and it’s a cacophony of shouting and laughing and crying and Jeff telling the cameras they will be right back after the break.

    Billy steps back a little, wiping his eyes, watching Jen bask in her win. “Tough break, huh?”

    Billy looks up to see Agatha, just as she slings an arm over Billy’s shoulder. She looks great, wearing a crisp suit with no shirt under it. He’s sure certain fans in the audience are going to have very reasonable reactions to that wardrobe choice.

    “Nah, I’m happy for her,” Billy says honestly.

    “Of course you are,” she says, rolling her eyes. But there’s no bite to it. Billy thinks about the lonely woman he watched on a screen at the Old Blood vs New Blood Reunion, sitting by herself during this moment of celebration, and then the woman next to him now, grinning through her loss.

    “Hey,” Billy says, emboldened by the energy in the room. “My mom’s actually in the audience if you wanna say hi.”

    “Fuck no,” Agatha says cheerliy.

    “You know, you guys really have more in common than you think,” Billy says.

    “Aren’t you such a wise teen,” Agatha mocks.

    “I’m 20 now.”

    “Well, excuse me, Mr. Two Whole Decades, I have to go congratulate my dear friend Jennifer Kale on winning Survivor.”

    “Sure, you do,” Billy says, laughing.

    He watches as Agatha, true to her word, cuts through the throngs of people to give Jen her proverbial flowers. Billy’s sure she’s saying something inappropriately bitchy, but Jen just laughs, the nonchalance of a newly minted millionaire.

    “We’re back in 60,” someone on the crew yells, and suddenly all of them are being arranged to sit where they are supposed to sit, Jen closest to Jeff, then Billy, then Rio, which places her on the bench next to Agatha. She leans down to whisper something in Agatha’s ear, who laughs, rests her head on Rio’s shoulder, as they are counted back to air, with a 3… 2… and —

    “What a season!” Jeff shouts to the camera. Up close, Billy can see he looks a little strained. It can’t still be the same stuff he was pissy about during the finale, can it? “We had ups, downs, people giving away immunity like it was their job, and of course, our wonderful winner, Jen.” The crowd cheers for Jen, as they should. It’s hard to feel bitter about losing a game when the winner is sitting beside him, glowing. Jeff prompts her to talk about strategy and she does, talking about always being the dominant alliance, making sure that she knew how to bounce back from moves, and, “honestly, selling the hell out of myself at final tribal.” “And what a final tribal it was,” Jeff says, jaw tight. “Billy, you came on here as a superfan of the game. Can you tell me if any tribal council you’ve seen on TV was like that one that aired tonight?”

    “Oh, absolutely not,” Billy says. “I don’t think there’s been anything like that in Survivor herstory.” He turns to Rio next to him, the energy in the studio making him brave and silly. “A lot more kissing than I remember seeing on TV.”

    At that, there’s a deafening roar from the crowd. The cameras tighten in on the closeness of Agatha and Rio, which means they miss Jeff rolling his eyes.

    “So,” Jeff says, like it’s the last thing he wants to ask in the world. “The people have been—I’m not exaggerating—begging to know the timeline of…” He gestures vaguely at Agatha and Rio. “…this.”

    “Timeline?” Agatha asks, pretending to be confused. Her hand, visibly, is on Rio’s thigh. “To what could you be referring?”

    “I just met this woman yesterday,” Rio deadpans.

    “You know what they say about lesbians moving fast,” Agatha jokes to the audience. “Met yesterday, married today. Am I right, Jeff?” Jeff doesn't take the bait.

    “Moving on,” he starts. “Jen, what did—”

    “If I may, Jeffrey,” Agatha cuts in, not waiting for an answer. “Being reunited with my banging wife really did put things into perspective.” “I’m sure it did,” Jeff mutters.

    “I’ve been a fool,” Agatha says, hand over her heart. Billy tries not to laugh. She’s really hamming it up tonight. “Silly ol’ Agatha, trying to win a million dollars, when the real prize was right here all along.” She puts her arm around Rio, and then kisses her soundly. The crowd cheers. Billy wonders if they buy it.

    “A million dollars is still pretty nice,” Jen counters, once Agatha has separated herself from Rio’s mouth. “So happy for you, though.” “Oh sure, Jennifer,” Agatha says, grinning through smudged lipstick. “I used to be like you, caring only about money and fame, but some of us found something deeper on the island.”

    Jen leans forward, toward Agatha. Billy is a little afraid of being in the crossfire here.

    “Oh yeah?” Jen asks. “You think you’re so special for having your little Survivor girlfriend?”

    “Wife,” Agatha corrects at the same time Rio says, “I’m right here.” “Well some of us,” Jen says with a grin, “managed to get a million dollars and a girlfriend out of this, so…”

    “Oh, yeah right,” Agatha scoffs. “Very funny.”

    “Not a joke,” Jen says smugly. “Right, Alice?”

    Jen, still grinning, turns behind her, hand outstretched to Alice, who takes it.

    “I mean, we haven’t actually labeled it yet,” Alice says with an easy shrug. “But you aren’t exactly the only couple to come out of this season.” “No way,” Billy murmurs. He’s gooped, what can he say? He’s also a little miffed no one told him, but it’s hard to stay mad when he is now sitting directly in between two beautiful Survivor lesbian couples. What could be better than this?

    “I can’t believe you are stealing my thunder on this,” Agatha snaps. “You’re stealing my thunder,” Jen argues. “I fucking won this show.” “This is live television,” Jeff bellows. Then he gets his big TV guy smile on and turns to face the cameras. “Tensions are high, emotions running over, people are falling in love left and right. Only on Survivor. We’ll be right back.”

    As soon as the cameras cut, he turns back to the contestants. ”Get it the fuck together, ladies.”

    ”Oh, I do not like the way he called us ladies,” Jen says. “Tell me about it,” Agatha grumbles.

    “Pretty sexist of you, Jeff,” Rio says flatly.

    “Unbelievable,” Jeff says, throwing his hands up as he storms off the set. “Do you think he’ll be back?” Billy asks.

    “Oh, don’t worry about it, kid,” Agatha says, “he just needs a little pick me up.” She taps the side of her nose.

    Billy’s eyebrows shoot up. “Seriously?”

    ”How could you have missed how coked up Jeff is?” Rio asks. Billy still isn’t sure if she likes him. “It’s, like, his whole deal.” “Oh, go easy on the teen, honey,” Agatha says.

    “I’m 20!”

    “I think we could all go a little easier on Jeff,” Lilia chimes in from Agatha’s other side. “He has a lot of stress.”

    “Yeah, I’ve heard he’s taking an old flame to Sweden tomorrow to help manage that stress,” Jen says slyly.

    “Jennifer,” Lilia warns.

    ”We’re back in five!” A producer calls. “We’ll be doing interviews in the audience, so will need quiet on set.”

    “Good luck with that,” Rio mutters.

    Billy watches the camera set up change to focus on the audience. There’s a set up framing Lorna Wu, looking ever so cool and collected, despite parts of her daughter’s romantic life that may or may not have just been exposed on TV. They are also doing a setup on Monica Rambeau, who seems to be having a conversation from the audience to the stage with both her wife and her daughter. And then, of course, there’s Billy’s mom. She’s sitting next to Tommy, listening intently to something he’s rambling about, probably some HBO prestige drama.

    Billy swallows a little as the cameras close in on his mom, as the producer keeps counting down the time. Not that he doesn’t think his mom will be great, it’s just that Survivor kind of brings out her… intensity. “Sixty seconds,” the producer calls. “Eyes on Probst?”

    “I’m right here, Jeremy,” Jeff says, appearing from nowhere to clap the producer rather aggressively on the back. He rubs his nose a little. “Great!” Jeremy says, “we’re gonna have you—”

    “In the audience,” Jeff finishes easily. “This isn’t my first rodeo, you know! Let’s talk to some moms!”

    Jeremy nods. Jeff winks at him and gives him a thumbs up before leaping up into the audience.

    “Alright,” Jeremy says a little shakily. “Quiet on set everyone, we are back in 3…2…” He points at Jeff.

    “Welcome back to the Survivor: Road to Redemption live reunion!” Jeff says cheerfully, like proverbial steam wasn’t blowing out of his ears just five minutes ago. “One of my favorite things about this season is that half of the contestants had a connection to some of the most infamous Survivor players of all time. And you know it wouldn’t be a reunion if we didn’t talk to some of them.”

    Jeff grins at the camera as it follows him up a few rows until he is standing right next to Billy’s mom.

    “We have Wanda Maximoff here, the winner of Old Blood vs New Blood and proud mom of our runner-up Billy.”

    There’s a break of applause, which Billy thinks is 90% for his mom and 10% for him, but he’ll take it.

    “You’re right Jeff,” Wanda says, with an easy smile. “I am very proud of Billy. Couldn’t be prouder.”

    Billy breathes out a little.

    “As we all remember,” Jeff tees up, “almost seven years and 13 seasons ago, you were announced the winner over your infamous rival Agatha Harkness.”

    “I don’t know if I’d say infamous—”

    “And yet, this season, she bafflingly gave up immunity to your own flesh and blood. How does something like that happen?”

    “Well, Jeff,” Wanda says slowly, but Billy knows her well enough to tell that it’s an effort to keep her tone light and conversational. Then she smiles directly at him. “Billy is something else. He has the power to melt the heart of even some very… difficult people.”

    “Aw,” Billy says.

    “Difficult people?” Rio says from Billy’s right. Somehow her mic is still live. Well, not somehow, the point of these things is for people to fight. “Watch out there, Wanda.”

    Billy puts his head in his hands. He would really love it if no one called his mother a cunt on live television.

    “I don’t want to rehash old drama,” Wanda says with a tight smile. “I’m just very proud of Billy.”

    “I’m sure you are,” Rio says with a sneer.

    Agatha, practically draped all over Rio on the bench, very loudly whispers in her ear, “get her, sweetheart.”

    “Can we not?” Billy says, exasperatedly. “Yes, my mom beat Agatha at Survivor. Yes, certain people feel a certain type of way about this. And not just these guys.” He turns to the audience, those late night comments he read online popping unbidden into his mind. “Some fans are also, like, really mean to her for no reason. So lay off her, okay? Get over it!” There’s a moment of silence. Billy certainly didn’t think he would have a freak out at the reunion. He feels his face go a little red. “You heard him,” Jeff says jovially from the audience. “Let’s all stop being mean. Even to me! Anyway!” He seamlessly hops down a couple steps to his next victim. “Lorna Wu, everyone! Now Lorna, could we ever get you back on this show?”

    And so it goes. Billy breathes out.

    “Hey,” Agatha whispers, mic thankfully not live anymore, “I’m sorry my wife is being such a bitch. I don’t know what has gotten into her” “Oh, fuck off,” Rio says, but she’s smiling. “It’s nothing personal, kid.” Billy really doesn’t think that’s true, but whatever. They aren’t on the island anymore. And besides, maybe he can actually use this rare moment where he has Agatha and Rio’s undivided attention.

    “Hey,” he says, “what are you guys doing after this?”

    The Night After the Reunion “So here’s how the timing works,” Billy is explaining to Sharon. “So the finale aired on the East Coast at eight o’clock, but that was only five here. So now it’s 8pm on the West Coast, and the finale just started airing.” ”This woman is 100 years old and doesn't understand time zones?” Agatha loudly whispers in Rio’s ear.

    Rio laughs. She feels a little high, languishing in the back of a massive car that CBS somehow procured for them. Agatha’s mouth is on her ear, her hand is on Rio’s thigh and they are fucking free. There are no more secrets from the public, no more questions of who won what or who is married to whom, it’s all out in the open now.

    “So by the time we get to the bar,” Billy is somehow still explaining, “it will only be maybe 20 minutes into the finale. So the people there will have no idea who won or anything that happened at the last final tribal.” Correction: Rio is about two hours away from everything being all out in the open.

    “Oh, wow,” Sharon says. “And where are we going again?”

    Rio enjoys watching Billy try not to roll his eyes. The apple doesn’t fall as far as he wants everyone to believe.

    “We’re going to a gay bar where they do live Survivor watch parties,” Billy explains faux-calmly. “I’ve been talking to the hosts online for the last few weeks and I said I would try to help them get some special guests. Honestly, I can’t believe I got so many special guests.”

    It is a little ridiculous, Rio is the first one to admit. She wouldn’t even be here if Agatha didn’t love to humor the kid. She would much rather crack open a bottle of champagne with only Agatha in the swanky hotel that CBS is paying for. But here she is, in the back of a car with her fellow Survivor contestants, with even more of them trailing them in another massive car. Plus some additional stragglers, with Nicky sitting on Agatha’s other side (wonderful), Wanda beside Billy (terrible), and Jen’s sister sticking her head out of the sunroof (neutral).

    “How long do we have to stay?” Rio whispers into Agatha’s ear. “Just until you’ve marked your territory,” Agatha whispers back, ever so briefly biting Rio’s ear in the process.

    On second thought, Rio is simply thrilled to be here.

    The cars take them to the back of the bar, where a drag king very enthusiastically greets Billy at the back entrance before leading their very conspicuous party to a huge corner booth in the back of the bar that’s been cordoned off from the public with one of those red velvet ropes. Despite the booth taking up significant real estate, it’s a tight squeeze. Rio takes it upon herself to slide up to sit on the back of the booth, positioning herself so Agatha sitting on the seat between Rio’s legs.

    “I thought you didn’t want us to be ‘seen as a couple’” Agatha says with a grin, leaning back between Rio’s thighs.

    She’s still wearing what she wore at the reunion. Rio takes a moment just to admire her from this angle, before sliding a hand down Agatha’s sternum. “Let them see,” she whispers. “I’m getting impatient.”

    “Good,” Agatha says.

    Rio reluctantly turns her attention to the plethora of TVs blasting Survivor around the bar, where herself and Agatha are the only two people left in the final immunity challenge.

    “I’m glad you’re here,” Agatha says onscreen.

    At the time, Rio thought Agatha was taunting her, was using her. And she was. But also… Rio looks up at the screens, at multiple close ups of Agatha’s face, at the way the raw honesty in her voice booms through the air.

    “Agatha,” Rio’s on screen counterpart says, “don’t.”

    Speaking of voices, Jesus Christ, her own voice sounds like it was muddled with six years of heartbreak. Which, well, it was, but these people don’t need to know that.

    “Oh my god,” Rio hears someone coo behind her, “I need them to get back together.”

    “Uh,” their companion says, “I’m pretty sure they do.”

    Then Rio feels two pairs of eyes on her. Good.

    “No way,” the first voice hisses. “That’s them.”

    “Unless the drag queens here have gotten really good…”

    The two awed queers are interrupted by a collective gasp from the room as Rio watches herself onscreen, so naive, so sad, lose her footing just a little and tumble into the water.

    “Agatha wins immunity!” Jeff calls.

    The bar explodes with cheers and yells and chants of Agatha and top three and we did it, the community experiencing a rare moment of alignment over the joy of Agatha winning immunity.

    Rio remembers that afternoon, through a haze of her heartbreak, remembers the visceral pain of losing yet another thing to Agatha, of how pathetic it felt to have everything always come back to this one woman. Now, Rio couldn’t be more elated that everything comes back to this one woman.

    “They love you,” she yells at Agatha over the noise.

    Agatha is looking out at the crowd cheering for her. Rio isn’t sure if it’s because they are so physically close, or just that she is intimately familiar with every piece of Agatha’s face, but Rio can see the vulnerability in how Agatha blinks, how touched she is by people actually rooting for her, loudly and unashamedly.

    Agatha looks up to Rio, raises her eyebrows. “Kind of makes you want to go back there.”

    “Don’t push it,” Rio says.

    Before the reunion, they had a deja vu-inducing conversation with Charlie where he practically begged them to return for another themed season. Rio said she would think about it.

    “I’ll talk it over with my wife,” Agatha said to Charlie, but Rio knew she wanted it again, knew she craved it out there, craved the high of the game. But Rio also knew the game was what tore them apart for years. And she couldn’t—she can’t—

    “Look at that,” Nicky says now from Agatha’s side, looking out on the cheering crowd. “Not so much of a villain now, are you?”

    Agatha waves a hand, but Rio can see the light in her eyes when she looks at him.

    “Not too loud,” Agatha says with a warm smile, “I have a reputation to uphold.”

    But Agatha’s reputation is more than intact. As soon as the show cuts to commercial, the host of the watch party gets on a mic, beaming and sweating through his makeup.

    “Talk about a fucking challenge win,” he screams into the mic, only for the bar to explode with applause again. “And a perfect tee up for our next performer. You know her, you love her. The queen of villains, the villain of queens, Gagatha Harness.”

    Rio lets out a loud laugh.

    “A double pun?” Agatha mutters. “I deserve better than that.” “Oh I love her,” Billy coos.

    “Of course you do,” Rio says.

    She leans back and has a very specific experience that is usually reserved for the Jay-Zs and Travis Kelces of the world, which is watching a drag performer do a really fucking solid impression of her wife. “She’s really good,” Rio whispers to Agatha, as Gagatha does a lip sync of “I Will Survive" interspersed with sound bites from Agatha’s time on Survivor, while doing some pretty impressive acrobatics.

    “Alright,” real Agatha says, “she’s no me.”

    “Yeah,” Rio says, “if you could put your legs behind your head like that…” “Fuck you,” Agatha says, and Rio laughs through the rest of the performance.

    By the time Gagatha has wrapped up her performance, Rio is out twenty bucks and the whole bar is definitely aware of their table. Phones are out and people are whispering and, unlike with that bitch today in the so-called farmers market, Rio kind of likes it. She drops a kiss to Agatha’s head, just to make them gasp.

    When the show comes back on, Rio spends more time watching the audience than the show itself. They really do love it, all these freaky little gay people, hanging onto every word said out there, from people who were sleep deprived and starving and not in their right mind.

    On screen, Agatha is trying to teach Billy how to make fire and he is majorly sucking at it. It’s all standard Survivor talk until Agatha happens to drop the little tidbit of their marital status.

    “Wife?” Billy shrieks onscreen, and that scream is echoed by dozens of gay people.

    “So dramatic,” Rio teases, as heads turn to look at them. “It just slipped out,” Agatha says, soaking in the attention directed at them. She looks up to Rio, radiant.

    With the way they are sitting, Rio is reminded of an older season of Survivor, that one All Stars with little Peter, where he was always climbing the damn trees, and the camera captured one moment where he was hanging from a tree and MJ wandered into the woods and kissed him while he was still upside down.

    At the time, it was called the most iconic kiss on reality television. And it’s about to be usurped in about 15 minutes.

    Sitting above Agatha on a sticky booth, looking down into her eyes, Rio really does get the appeal of the angle.

    Somehow, she resists the urge to kiss Agatha just yet. She’ll give it about 15 minutes.

    “Just wait until tribal,” Agatha says, reading Rio’s thoughts. “These people will lose their minds.”

    Sure enough, when tribal comes around, the room waits with bated breath. “It is time to vote,” Jeff says on screen. Murmurs start, speculation of who will get sent home, if it will come down to fire.

    “Jeff, wait,” Agatha calls on screen, so dramatic yet again. The bar goes silent, as Agatha takes off her necklace, and hangs it around Billy’s neck.

    Rio has to admit, the editors did amazing at cutting out Jeff’s mental breakdown for TV, it goes right from the necklace transfer to Rio standing up on her feet, her eyes wet with unshed tears, asking Agatha, “why? Why him?”

    God, Rio’s least favorite thing about watching this season back is how much of a fucking loser she was. Just trust your damn wife, Rio wants to yell at herself through the screen. Hindsight really is 20/20 when the woman in question is leaning back against Rio, warm between her legs. “I’ll go tally the votes,” Jeff says.

    The bar is abuzz, everyone glancing back at their table, whispering why would she do this and this doesn't make sense and I want an all dyke top three and Agatha can’t go home, she’s in my fantasy league. The voices of dissent rise when Agatha is voted off. Rio is busier looking at the faces onscreen. Billy crying and in awe, Jen just vaguely amused, and then there’s herself, righteous anger and devastation fighting for dominance as she gets up and confronts Agatha.

    “You just gave up everything for some kid,” she says onscreen, voice cracking.

    “This is when it gets good,” Agatha in the present, whispers to Nicky. Her hand squeezes Rio’s calf.

    On screen, Agatha cups Rio’s cheek. “No, my love,” she says. “I gave it all up for you.”

    The bar goes wild.

    “My love,” someone shouts, “holy shit, holy shit.”

    “They’re in love!”

    “They’re married and in love!”

    “I still can’t believe Agatha isn’t top three!”

    “Who cares about the stupid game, they are in love!”

    “They don’t even know,” Agatha whispers, leaning up again to look at Rio’s eyes.

    Agatha’s eyes are bright sea blue, and her mouth is smiling so wide that Rio feels her heart stall in her chest. It feels unreal that, as they speak, dozens of people are watching them in this bar, and millions more are watching them on TV.

    “This next part’s really good,” Billy say to Nicky, both of them framing Agatha in the booth.

    Jen rolls her eyes. “It’s not that good,” she grumbles, but the bite is taken out of her words by permanent smile she’s had on her face since winning. “Oh let them have it,” Lilia sighs, raptly watching the screen like she wasn’t there on the island with them.

    “Hey, don’t you have an early flight to catch tomorrow?” Alice asks Lilia, with a knowing grin.

    “What am I missing here?” Billy asks, and Jen and Alice just laugh. Rio doesn’t pay attention to any of them. Her eyes are only on Agatha. The dozens of people in this bar and millions of people watching Survivor are about to see right in front of their eyes that, after six years and 39 days and nine months, Agatha is finally hers.

    “One quick sec Jeff,” Agatha says on screen. Rio isn’t looking. Not when the real thing is right in front of her. “I’m not leaving this game without doing something I’ve wanted to do for 38 days.”

    And then, on screen and in real life, Agatha kisses Rio. And then, on screen and in real life, Rio takes Agatha’s head in her hands and kisses her and kisses her, breathing her in and loving her so much that she forgets they have an audience.

    When they pull apart, Agatha looks messy and smug and so beautiful that Rio can’t believe it.

    “So?” Agatha asks, breathless. They are surrounded by people, but the question is just for Rio. “One more time? You and me?”

    Rio leans back down, grinning into Agatha’s mouth. They both already know the answer.
